### Ultra Lowrate Image Compression with Semantic Residual Coding and Compression-aware Diffusion
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.08281v1)
- **Authors**: Anle Ke, Xu Zhang, Tong Chen, Ming Lu, Chao Zhou, Jiawen Gu, Zhan Ma
- **Abstract**: Existing multimodal large model-based image compression frameworks often rely on a fragmented integration of semantic retrieval, latent compression, and generative models, resulting in suboptimal performance in both reconstruction fidelity and coding efficiency. To address these challenges, we propose a residual-guided ultra lowrate image compression named ResULIC, which incorporates residual signals into both semantic retrieval and the diffusion-based generation process. Specifically, we introduce Semantic Residual Coding (SRC) to capture the semantic disparity between the original image and its compressed latent representation. A perceptual fidelity optimizer is further applied for superior reconstruction quality. Additionally, we present the Compression-aware Diffusion Model (CDM), which establishes an optimal alignment between bitrates and diffusion time steps, improving compression-reconstruction synergy. Extensive experiments demonstrate the effectiveness of ResULIC, achieving superior objective and subjective performance compared to state-of-the-art diffusion-based methods with - 80.7%, -66.3% BD-rate saving in terms of LPIPS and FID. Project page is available at https: //njuvision.github.io/ResULIC/.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces ResULIC, a novel residual-guided ultra-low bitrate image compression framework.  ResULIC addresses limitations in existing multimodal large model-based image compression methods that often suffer from fragmented integration of semantic retrieval, latent compression, and generative models. ResULIC incorporates residual signals into both semantic retrieval and the diffusion-based generation process.  It proposes Semantic Residual Coding (SRC) to capture semantic disparity between the original image and its compressed latent representation. A perceptual fidelity optimizer is applied to improve reconstruction quality.  The paper also introduces the Compression-aware Diffusion Model (CDM), which aligns bitrates and diffusion time steps for better compression-reconstruction synergy.  Experiments demonstrate that ResULIC outperforms state-of-the-art diffusion-based methods in both objective and subjective evaluations.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in the integration of semantic residual coding within a diffusion-based image compression framework and the introduction of a compression-aware diffusion model. While individual components like diffusion models and semantic retrieval exist, their synergistic combination and bitrate-adaptive design within ResULIC appear to be a novel contribution.  SRC is also a notable component, specifically designed for low-bitrate scenarios to reduce semantic redundancy. However, the use of existing components like GPT-4o does reduce the overall novelty level somewhat.

*   **Significance:** The significance of the work stems from its potential to enable high-quality image compression at extremely low bitrates. This is critical for applications with limited bandwidth, such as mobile devices, IoT devices, and streaming services in areas with poor network connectivity. The demonstrated improvement in perceptual quality and coding efficiency compared to existing methods signifies a meaningful advancement. The ablation studies are helpful in demonstrating the importance of each proposed module.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Performance:**  The experimental results showing substantial BD-rate savings in terms of LPIPS and FID are compelling evidence of ResULIC's effectiveness.
    *   **Architecture:** The design choices, such as the integration of SRC and CDM, address specific limitations in existing approaches.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper includes both objective and subjective evaluations, as well as ablation studies to justify the contributions of each component. Datasets are extensive and well known, which is ideal.
    *   **Adaptability:** There are tests to assess the adaptability of ResULIC in different circumstances with different base algorithms.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Complexity:** The system is complex, which may increase the encoding and decoding time. This is especially obvious for the perceptual optimizer.
    *   **Dependency on Pretrained Models:** The framework relies heavily on pretrained models like Stable Diffusion and GPT-4o, which limits the control over the model. The benefits of replacing GPT-4o with other large multimodal models may not be worth the trade in time and performance.

*   **Potential Impact:** The work can have a significant impact on areas where bandwidth is limited and high-quality image transmission is essential. It is likely to inspire further research into integrating semantic information and diffusion models for advanced image compression. It could also influence the design of future image compression standards and codecs.

**Justification of Score:**

Considering the above points, the paper makes a solid contribution. While the overall architecture is somewhat complex and builds upon existing components, the specific combination of SRC and CDM, along with the promising experimental results, demonstrates a worthwhile advancement in the field of ultra-low bitrate image compression. There are components of ResULIC that may be adapted to other models, but there are also trade-offs with pretrained models.

### A Practical Introduction to Deep Reinforcement Learning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.08295v1)
- **Authors**: Yinghan Sun, Hongxi Wang, Hua Chen, Wei Zhang
- **Abstract**: Deep reinforcement learning (DRL) has emerged as a powerful framework for solving sequential decision-making problems, achieving remarkable success in a wide range of applications, including game AI, autonomous driving, biomedicine, and large language models. However, the diversity of algorithms and the complexity of theoretical foundations often pose significant challenges for beginners seeking to enter the field. This tutorial aims to provide a concise, intuitive, and practical introduction to DRL, with a particular focus on the Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO) algorithm, which is one of the most widely used and effective DRL methods. To facilitate learning, we organize all algorithms under the Generalized Policy Iteration (GPI) framework, offering readers a unified and systematic perspective. Instead of lengthy theoretical proofs, we emphasize intuitive explanations, illustrative examples, and practical engineering techniques. This work serves as an efficient and accessible guide, helping readers rapidly progress from basic concepts to the implementation of advanced DRL algorithms.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a summary and critical evaluation of the paper based on the provided text.

**Summary:**

The paper is a tutorial introduction to deep reinforcement learning (DRL), specifically focusing on the Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO) algorithm. It aims to provide a concise, intuitive, and practical guide for beginners, emphasizing key concepts and implementation details rather than lengthy theoretical proofs.  It organizes algorithms under the Generalized Policy Iteration (GPI) framework for a unified perspective and stresses practical engineering techniques. The tutorial covers fundamental RL concepts, value estimation methods (Monte Carlo, Temporal Difference learning), policy gradient methods, Generalized Advantage Estimation (GAE), and culminates in the complete PPO algorithm.  The paper aims to be an accessible resource, helping readers progress quickly from basic concepts to advanced DRL algorithm implementation.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper does not claim to introduce new algorithms or theoretical breakthroughs. Its novelty lies in its *pedagogical approach* and *organization*.  It aims to synthesize existing knowledge in a more accessible and practically-oriented manner. Organizing DRL algorithms within the GPI framework is a useful way to provide a high-level structure for understanding relationships between algorithms. However, GPI is a fairly well-established concept, so this is not truly a novel contribution. Focusing on the PPO algorithm is a practical choice, as it's a widely used and effective method. The focus on intuition and engineering techniques, rather than theoretical proofs, may be helpful for practitioners but does not represent a scientific contribution in itself.

**Significance:** The paper's significance rests on its potential to *lower the barrier to entry* for individuals wanting to learn DRL. A clear, concise, and practical tutorial can be highly valuable in a rapidly evolving field. If the tutorial is well-written and the implementation guidance is effective, it could contribute to wider adoption and understanding of DRL techniques. It emphasizes the most useful aspects for practitioners, so that should lead to a greater potential for people to pick it up. However, a critical element for the paper's significance will depend on it being well-received by it's target audience. This will depend in large part on its clarity of explanation and quality of its engineering guidance.

**Strengths:**

*   **Focus on practicality:** The emphasis on implementation details and engineering techniques is a significant strength, as many introductory materials lack this practical focus.
*   **Clear organization (GPI framework):** Organizing algorithms under the GPI framework offers a helpful high-level structure for understanding the relationships between them.
*   **Concise scope (PPO):** Focusing on PPO allows for a deeper dive into a single, important algorithm, rather than a superficial overview of many.
*   **Accessible language and focus on intuition:** A strong emphasis on intuitive explanations can make the material more approachable for beginners.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Lack of truly novel content:**  The paper primarily synthesizes existing knowledge. It does not provide new theoretical insights or algorithmic innovations.
*   **Reliance on existing frameworks:** The GPI framework is already well-established.
*   **Evaluation:**  Without evidence of real adoption or impact, it's hard to judge the true impact.
*   **Dependency on quality:** The actual value critically hinges on the quality and comprehensiveness of the provided explanations and practical advice, which the provided text does not allow me to fully assess.
*   **Scope Limitedness:** It focuses solely on the PPO algorithm, potentially missing broader considerations.

**Justification for Score:**

Based on the above analysis, the paper's primary contribution is *pedagogical*. While it may be a valuable resource for those learning DRL, it doesn't present new research findings or significantly advance the state of the art. Therefore, it cannot score highly in terms of novelty or overall significance to the field. If the tutorial is extraordinarily well-written and widely adopted, the score could potentially increase, but based solely on the abstract and outline, a score of around the median reflects the paper's contribution.

**Score: 6**

**Rigorous Rationale:** The score of 6 reflects the tutorial's *potential* value to the DRL community as an accessible learning resource, especially given the focus on PPO and the practical nature of the explanations. However, it is *not* a scientific breakthrough. The novelty is primarily in the presentation and organization of existing knowledge, not the creation of new knowledge. Therefore, it scores above average, but not highly. The actual value depends entirely on the quality of the completed tutorial, but based on the provided context, a 6 seems to be the right spot. The tutorial's significance relies on its ability to effectively bridge the gap between basic concepts and practical implementation, a feat whose quality is hard to judge without the full text.

### Efficient Unstructured Pruning of Mamba State-Space Models for Resource-Constrained Environments
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.08299v1)
- **Authors**: Ibne Farabi Shihab, Sanjeda Akter, Anuj Sharma
- **Abstract**: State-space models (SSMs), particularly the Mamba architecture, have emerged as powerful alternatives to Transformers for sequence modeling, offering linear-time complexity and competitive performance across diverse tasks. However, their large parameter counts pose significant challenges for deployment in resource-constrained environments. We propose a novel unstructured pruning framework tailored for Mamba models that achieves up to 70\% parameter reduction while retaining over 95\% of the original performance. Our approach integrates three key innovations: (1) a gradient-aware magnitude pruning technique that combines weight magnitude and gradient information to identify less critical parameters, (2) an iterative pruning schedule that gradually increases sparsity to maintain model stability, and (3) a global pruning strategy that optimizes parameter allocation across the entire model. Through extensive experiments on WikiText-103, Long Range Arena, and ETT time-series benchmarks, we demonstrate significant efficiency gains with minimal performance degradation. Our analysis of pruning effects on Mamba's components reveals critical insights into the architecture's redundancy and robustness, enabling practical deployment in resource-constrained settings while broadening Mamba's applicability.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel unstructured pruning framework tailored for Mamba state-space models (SSMs). It aims to address the challenge of deploying these models in resource-constrained environments, given their high parameter counts. The framework combines three key innovations: a gradient-aware magnitude pruning technique (considering both weight magnitude and gradient information), an iterative pruning schedule with a cubic progression (gradually increasing sparsity), and a global pruning strategy (optimizing parameter allocation across the entire model). The authors conduct extensive experiments on several benchmarks (WikiText-103, Long Range Arena, ETT), demonstrating substantial parameter reduction (up to 70%) with minimal performance degradation (over 95% retention).  The paper also analyzes the effects of pruning on different Mamba components, revealing insights into the architecture's redundancy and robustness.  Finally, the paper provides extended evaluations including analyses of computational efficiency, robustness, and ablation studies, as well as implementation details.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's core strength lies in its adaptation and tailoring of existing pruning techniques to the specific architecture of Mamba SSMs.  While gradient-aware magnitude pruning, iterative pruning schedules, and global pruning strategies are individually known, their combination and adaptation to Mamba's recurrent nature and selective mechanisms constitute a novel contribution.  Specifically, the stability checks that enforce constraints of the eigenvalues are novel and necessary. Applying these techniques to a rapidly developing architectural space, like Mamba, is innovative because there is not yet a solid understanding of its redundancies.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a critical problem in deploying large language models, which are the computational resource demands for inference.  By successfully pruning Mamba models without significant performance loss, the authors make these models more practical for edge computing, mobile devices, and other resource-constrained environments. The insights into Mamba's component-wise importance are valuable for future architectural development and optimization.  The enhanced robustness of pruned models to input perturbations is a surprising and potentially significant finding that warrants further investigation.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Comprehensive experimental evaluation across diverse tasks and datasets.
    *   Thorough ablation studies justifying the design choices of the pruning framework.
    *   Detailed analysis of the impact of pruning on Mamba's components, providing valuable insights into the architecture.
    *   Clear presentation of the methodology and results.
    *   Open-source implementation makes the framework readily accessible.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   While the individual pruning techniques are well-established, the paper primarily focuses on *adaptation* rather than inventing entirely new pruning methods.
    *   The paper primarily uses the AdamW optimizer for fine-tuning. There is some prior work suggesting that *fully tuning* using a more modern or complex optimizer might yield significantly improved results.
    *   The stability metric and its use are explained in detail, but it's possible the same effect could be achieved in a more straightforward or efficient fashion that would remove complexity in the mask selection mechanism.
    *   The gains are demonstrated across a variety of language tasks, but it is not demonstrated whether that is generalizable to other unstructured pruning tasks that require retaining specialized or niche functionality (e.g. retaining specialized function calls in a code generation task).

*   **Potential Impact:** The paper is likely to have a significant impact on the field of efficient deep learning, particularly for state-space models. It provides a practical and effective solution for deploying Mamba models in resource-constrained environments, broadening their applicability. The insights into Mamba's architecture and the pruning process are valuable for future research in this area.

*   **Justification of Score:**

While the paper doesn't introduce fundamentally novel pruning algorithms, it provides a highly practical and insightful contribution by tailoring existing techniques to the specific architecture of Mamba models. The extensive experimental evaluation and in-depth analysis of pruning effects make this a significant contribution to the field. The performance improvements are noteworthy and are likely to encourage the adoption of Mamba models in a wider range of applications.  However, there is room for further innovation in terms of pruning algorithms and optimization strategies specific to SSMs, and there is some prior work that could be more directly compared against. With the inclusion of the open-source code, the replicability is also improved, contributing to adoption and application. For this, it is awarded a slightly-higher-than-average score.

### Evaluating the Effectiveness of Black-Box Prompt Optimization as the Scale of LLMs Continues to Grow
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.08303v1)
- **Authors**: Ziyu Zhou, Yihang Wu, Jingyuan Yang, Zhan Xiao, Rongjun Li
- **Abstract**: Black-Box prompt optimization methods have emerged as a promising strategy for refining input prompts to better align large language models (LLMs), thereby enhancing their task performance. Although these methods have demonstrated encouraging results, most studies and experiments have primarily focused on smaller-scale models (e.g., 7B, 14B) or earlier versions (e.g., GPT-3.5) of LLMs. As the scale of LLMs continues to increase, such as with DeepSeek V3 (671B), it remains an open question whether these black-box optimization techniques will continue to yield significant performance improvements for models of such scale. In response to this, we select three well-known black-box optimization methods and evaluate them on large-scale LLMs (DeepSeek V3 and Gemini 2.0 Flash) across four NLU and NLG datasets. The results show that these black-box prompt optimization methods offer only limited improvements on these large-scale LLMs. Furthermore, we hypothesize that the scale of the model is the primary factor contributing to the limited benefits observed. To explore this hypothesis, we conducted experiments on LLMs of varying sizes (Qwen 2.5 series, ranging from 7B to 72B) and observed an inverse scaling law, wherein the effectiveness of black-box optimization methods diminished as the model size increased.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the effectiveness of black-box prompt optimization methods as LLM scale increases.  The authors evaluate three popular black-box optimization techniques (EvoPrompt, ProTeGi, and BPO) on large-scale LLMs (DeepSeek V3 and Gemini 2.0 Flash) across four NLU and NLG datasets.  Their results indicate that these methods provide only limited performance improvements on large models. Furthermore, they demonstrate an inverse scaling law, where the effectiveness of black-box optimization diminishes as the LLM size increases, using the Qwen-2.5 series of models (7B to 72B parameters).

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Relevant and Timely Research Question:**  The paper addresses a crucial question in the LLM field: Do existing prompt optimization techniques still work as models scale? This is directly relevant as researchers and practitioners increasingly use very large models.
    *   **Empirical Evaluation with Large Models:**  The paper tests optimization methods on genuinely large LLMs (DeepSeek V3, Gemini 2.0 Flash, and the Qwen 2.5 series), which is a significant contribution as much prior work focused on smaller models or older generations of LLMs.
    *   **Clear and Well-Defined Experiments:**  The experimental setup is clearly described, and the datasets and evaluation metrics are appropriate for the task.
    *   **Identification of an Inverse Scaling Law:** The discovery and documentation of the inverse scaling law provides valuable insight into the interaction between model size and prompt optimization techniques.
    *   **Analysis of Optimized Prompts:** The analysis of the prompts created by optimization methods provides insight into the types of changes that are (and are not) effective for larger models.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Scope of Optimization Techniques:** While the paper tests three popular methods, the black-box prompt optimization landscape is vast.  The findings might not generalize to all black-box techniques.
    *   **Limited Dataset Diversity:**  The experiments focus on a relatively small set of datasets. Expanding the evaluation to a wider range of tasks and domains would strengthen the conclusions.
    *   **Lack of Deeper Mechanistic Explanation:** The paper shows the inverse scaling law but doesn't fully explain *why* it occurs. More in-depth investigation into the internal representations and decision-making processes of LLMs during prompt optimization could be beneficial. The "minor synonym substitutions are unlikely to have a significant impact on large-scale LLMs" argument is suggestive, but not conclusive.
    *   **Lack of Statistical Significance Testing:** The improvements reported on large models are small, and the paper doesn't report statistical significance tests. This makes it difficult to determine if the observed improvements are real or due to random chance.

*   **Novelty and Significance:** The paper offers new empirical evidence challenging the conventional wisdom that prompt optimization is always beneficial. The inverse scaling law observation is a significant contribution.  The finding has implications for how we approach prompt engineering with large models, suggesting that more sophisticated or different types of optimization might be needed. However, given the limitations above (lack of statistical analysis, limited breadth of prompt techniques and datasets), the findings are not fully conclusive.

*   **Potential Influence:**  The paper could influence future research by shifting focus away from generic black-box optimization towards more targeted approaches designed for specific model architectures or tasks. It highlights the need to understand how model scale interacts with different optimization strategies.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a significant and timely empirical study, identifying an interesting and potentially important inverse scaling law. However, the limitations, specifically the lack of statistical testing and depth in its explanation of the inverse scaling law prevents a higher score. The finding may have broad implications in the LLM area. The paper is well-written and clearly presents its findings.

### Benchmarking AI scientists in omics data-driven biological research
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.08341v1)
- **Authors**: Erpai Luo, Jinmeng Jia, Yifan Xiong, Xiangyu Li, Xiaobo Guo, Baoqi Yu, Lei Wei, Xuegong Zhang
- **Abstract**: The rise of large language models and multi-agent systems has sparked growing interest in AI scientists capable of autonomous biological research. However, existing benchmarks either focus on reasoning without data or on data analysis with predefined statistical answers, lacking realistic, data-driven evaluation settings. Here, we introduce the Biological AI Scientist Benchmark (BaisBench), a benchmark designed to assess AI scientists' ability to generate biological discoveries through data analysis and reasoning with external knowledge. BaisBench comprises two tasks: cell type annotation on 31 expert-labeled single-cell datasets, and scientific discovery through answering 198 multiple-choice questions derived from the biological insights of 41 recent single-cell studies. Systematic experiments on state-of-the-art AI scientists and LLM agents showed that while promising, current models still substantially underperform human experts on both tasks. We hope BaisBench will fill this gap and serve as a foundation for advancing and evaluating AI models for scientific discovery. The benchmark can be found at: https://github.com/EperLuo/BaisBench.
- **Summary**: Okay, I've read the paper. Here's a summary and a critical evaluation:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces BaisBench, a benchmark designed to evaluate the capabilities of AI scientists in omics data-driven biological research.  Recognizing that existing benchmarks either focus on reasoning without data or on data analysis with predefined statistical answers, the authors aim to create a more realistic, data-driven evaluation setting. BaisBench consists of two tasks: 1) **Cell Type Annotation (BAIS-CTA)**, which involves annotating cell types in 31 expert-labeled single-cell datasets, and 2) **Scientific Discovery (BAIS-SD)**, which requires answering 198 multiple-choice questions derived from the biological insights of 41 recent single-cell studies.  The paper then presents experimental results using state-of-the-art AI scientists and LLM agents, demonstrating that while these models show promise, they still significantly underperform human experts on both tasks. The authors conclude that BaisBench can help advance and evaluate AI models for scientific discovery. They provide access to the benchmark on GitHub.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Addresses a Real Need:** The paper correctly identifies a gap in the evaluation of AI scientists for biological research.  Current benchmarks often lack the complexity and realism of real-world omics data analysis, which requires not only computational skills but also strong domain knowledge and reasoning abilities. BaisBench attempts to bridge this gap.
*   **Well-Defined Tasks:** The two tasks, cell type annotation and scientific discovery (answering multiple-choice questions based on research papers), are relevant and well-defined within the context of biological research.
*   **Use of Real Data:**  The benchmark is based on real single-cell transcriptomic datasets and biological insights from published research, increasing its relevance and ecological validity.
*   **Hierarchical Annotation Metric (uHAF):** The introduction of uHAF is a significant contribution to the community in terms of evaluating cell-type annotation algorithms. It allows for a more detailed and informative scoring of cell annotation that takes the hierarchical nature of cell identity into account.
*   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper provides a clear comparison of AI scientist performance against human experts and classical tools like CellTypist.
*   **Open Availability:** Making the benchmark publicly available on GitHub is crucial for fostering further research and development in this area.
*   **Aviary Implementation** The use of Aviary to enable agentic reasoning and tool usage is a very positive aspect of the implementation of the Bais-SD task. It allows the AI scientists to explore datasets and identify the correct answers based on data analysis.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Novelty in Task Design:** The individual tasks themselves (cell type annotation and multiple-choice question answering) are not inherently novel. The novelty lies in combining them in a benchmark specifically targeted at evaluating AI scientists in biological research.
*   **Multiple-Choice Question Limitations:** The scientific discovery task relies on multiple-choice questions. While this provides a structured evaluation, it limits the ability of AI scientists to generate truly novel hypotheses or insights.  It also might be easier to reverse-engineer the correct answer, especially considering that, from an assessment point of view, questions were specifically curated in order to be answerable from the data. A more open-ended evaluation, perhaps involving expert assessment of AI-generated summaries or hypotheses, might be more insightful.
*   **Performance of LLMs is not unexpected** Given the limited capabilities of LLMs in data-driven tasks, as well as their tendency to hallucinate, it is not surprising that the results of Bais-SD task are low. There is perhaps a point to be made on whether this part of the benchmark is as useful as Bais-CTA.
*  **CellTypist as Gold Standard** While it is a great tool to use, using a cell-type annotation method that is considered "classical" as a gold standard may provide a biased perspective on the quality of current methods and may benefit some of the methods that rely on this approach. It would be beneficial to use other orthogonal tools to generate cell-type annotation for Bais-CTA and see whether it changes the overall conclusions.

**Significance:**

The paper's significance lies in its potential to accelerate the development of AI scientists for biological research. By providing a realistic and challenging benchmark, BaisBench can help researchers identify the strengths and weaknesses of current AI models and guide future research directions. BaisBench can also facilitate the comparison of different AI approaches and track progress over time.

**Potential Influence:**

BaisBench could become a widely adopted benchmark in the field, similar to ImageNet in computer vision or GLUE in natural language processing. This could lead to significant advances in AI-driven biological research, with applications in drug discovery, personalized medicine, and fundamental biological understanding.

**Justification for Score:**

I am assigning a score of **7** because BaisBench is an important contribution to the field that addresses a real need and has the potential to significantly impact the development of AI scientists for biological research. The weaknesses, particularly the limitations of the multiple-choice question format and the relative lack of novelty in the individual tasks, prevent a higher score. The contribution will likely be most impactful as a standardized dataset and annotation scheme for single-cell data analysis tools.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### Alignment Drift in CEFR-prompted LLMs for Interactive Spanish Tutoring
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.08351v1)
- **Authors**: Mina Almasi, Ross Deans Kristensen-McLachlan
- **Abstract**: This paper investigates the potentials of Large Language Models (LLMs) as adaptive tutors in the context of second-language learning. In particular, we evaluate whether system prompting can reliably constrain LLMs to generate only text appropriate to the student's competence level. We simulate full teacher-student dialogues in Spanish using instruction-tuned, open-source LLMs ranging in size from 7B to 12B parameters. Dialogues are generated by having an LLM alternate between tutor and student roles with separate chat histories. The output from the tutor model is then used to evaluate the effectiveness of CEFR-based prompting to control text difficulty across three proficiency levels (A1, B1, C1). Our findings suggest that while system prompting can be used to constrain model outputs, prompting alone is too brittle for sustained, long-term interactional contexts - a phenomenon we term alignment drift. Our results provide insights into the feasibility of LLMs for personalized, proficiency-aligned adaptive tutors and provide a scalable method for low-cost evaluation of model performance without human participants.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores the feasibility of using system prompting to constrain Large Language Models (LLMs) to generate text at specific CEFR proficiency levels for Spanish language learners. The authors simulate teacher-student dialogues using LLMs and evaluate the effectiveness of CEFR-based prompts in maintaining the desired text difficulty over multiple turns. They find that while initial prompting can influence the output, its effect diminishes over time, a phenomenon they term "alignment drift." This suggests that prompting alone may not be sufficient for creating sustained, proficiency-aligned interactive language tutors. They also tested four models - Llama, Gemma, Mistral, and Qwen - finding performance differences between them. The study also presents a cost-effective method for evaluating LLM performance in interactive language learning scenarios through dialogue simulation.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a valuable contribution by investigating the *temporal aspect* of using LLMs as language tutors.  Much of the existing work focuses on single-turn interactions or short examples. The "alignment drift" finding is novel and important because it highlights a potential limitation of simple prompting strategies for long-term interactive learning. The use of simulated dialogues is also a good, though not entirely novel, approach to efficient evaluation. Few papers that I've seen analyze the drift of LLM outputs over multiple turns in simulated dialogue.

*   **Significance:** The research addresses a crucial question regarding the real-world applicability of LLMs in personalized language learning. If prompting proves insufficient to maintain consistent proficiency levels, more sophisticated control mechanisms (e.g., fine-tuning, dynamic prompt adjustment) will be needed. The findings directly impact the design and implementation of LLM-based language tutoring systems. The simple, scalable evaluation method they describe also has potential value.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Clear problem definition and research question.
    *   Systematic experimental design.
    *   Use of multiple LLMs for comparison.
    *   Introduction of the concept of "alignment drift."
    *   The discussion on the ethical concerns and limitations is thorough.
    *   The paper details a useful and scalable method for evaluating LLM performance.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The range of models tested, while competent, could be expanded.
    *   The complexity of the system prompts they are testing is relatively basic; more refined prompt engineering (or iterative prompt modification strategies) could perhaps improve the long-term stability.
    *   Reliance on automated metrics (readability, surprisal) for evaluation. While necessary for scalability, these metrics may not perfectly capture the nuanced aspects of language difficulty and appropriateness for L2 learners. Human evaluation is always preferable, if feasible, and that is acknowledged in the paper.
    *   The "student" LLM prompt is kept simple, which could influence overall dialogue dynamics.  A more sophisticated student model might create a more challenging or realistic tutoring scenario.

*   **Potential Influence:**  The paper's findings are likely to influence future research on LLM-based language tutoring by shifting the focus from simple prompting to more robust control and adaptation strategies. The method for simulating dialogues can also be adopted by other researchers for efficient evaluation of LLM performance.

*   **Rigorous Rationale:** While well-executed, the work has some limitations in scope (basic prompting, use of automatic metrics). The novelty is somewhat limited by previous work on prompting and dialogue simulation, but the specific investigation of *alignment drift* in CEFR-constrained language learning is a valuable contribution. The potential impact on the field of language tutoring is clear and significant.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Learning Like Humans: Advancing LLM Reasoning Capabilities via Adaptive Difficulty Curriculum Learning and Expert-Guided Self-Reformulation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.08364v1)
- **Authors**: Enci Zhang, Xingang Yan, Wei Lin, Tianxiang Zhang, Qianchun Lu
- **Abstract**: Despite impressive progress in areas like mathematical reasoning, large language models still face significant challenges in consistently solving complex problems. Drawing inspiration from key human learning strategies, we propose two novel strategies to enhance the capability of large language models to solve these complex problems. First, Adaptive Difficulty Curriculum Learning (ADCL) is a novel curriculum learning strategy that tackles the Difficulty Shift phenomenon (i.e., a model's perception of problem difficulty dynamically changes during training) by periodically re-estimating difficulty within upcoming data batches to maintain alignment with the model's evolving capabilities. Second, Expert-Guided Self-Reformulation (EGSR) is a novel reinforcement learning strategy that bridges the gap between imitation learning and pure exploration by guiding models to reformulate expert solutions within their own conceptual framework, rather than relying on direct imitation, fostering deeper understanding and knowledge assimilation. Extensive experiments on challenging mathematical reasoning benchmarks, using Qwen2.5-7B as the base model, demonstrate that these human-inspired strategies synergistically and significantly enhance performance. Notably, their combined application improves performance over the standard Zero-RL baseline by 10% on the AIME24 benchmark and 16.6% on AIME25.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces two novel strategies to improve the reasoning capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs), particularly in complex mathematical problem-solving. The first strategy, Adaptive Difficulty Curriculum Learning (ADCL), dynamically adjusts the training curriculum by periodically re-estimating the difficulty of upcoming data batches, addressing the "Difficulty Shift" phenomenon where the model's perception of difficulty changes during training. The second strategy, Expert-Guided Self-Reformulation (EGSR), encourages the model to internalize expert solutions by guiding it to reformulate them within its own conceptual framework, rather than simply imitating them. Experiments on mathematical reasoning benchmarks, using Qwen2.5-7B, demonstrate that combining ADCL and EGSR significantly enhances performance, surpassing standard Zero-RL baselines.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents two original techniques – ADCL and EGSR – that address limitations in existing LLM training paradigms. ADCL's dynamic difficulty adjustment offers a practical solution to the evolving perception of difficulty during training, while EGSR attempts to bridge the gap between imitation learning and on-policy exploration.
*   **Sound Motivation:** The authors clearly articulate the limitations of static curriculum learning and on-policy exploration in the context of LLM reasoning. The analogy to human learning provides a compelling motivation for the proposed strategies.
*   **Empirical Validation:** The experiments are well-designed and demonstrate the effectiveness of ADCL and EGSR on challenging mathematical reasoning benchmarks. The comparative analysis with standard Zero-RL, PCL, and off-policy approaches provides strong evidence for the benefits of the proposed techniques.
*   **Synergistic Combination:** The paper demonstrates that combining ADCL and EGSR leads to further improvements in performance, highlighting their synergistic effect.
*   **Insightful Analysis:** The analysis of the Difficulty Shift phenomenon, as well as the perplexity analysis used to justify the EGSR method is well conceived, providing insights into model behaviour and justifying the choice of approaches.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Computational Cost:** The paper doesn't fully address the computational overhead of ADCL, which involves periodically re-estimating the difficulty of upcoming data batches, as well as EGSR. While ADCL may be more efficient than full SPL, the cost of multiple passes over subsets of the data still warrants consideration.  EGSR similarly needs to be compared to IL to asses its cost/performance trade-off.
*   **Guidance Dependency:** The effectiveness of EGSR is dependent on the availability of high-quality expert solutions. The paper could benefit from a discussion of scenarios where such data is limited or noisy.
*   **Generality:** While the paper demonstrates strong results on mathematical reasoning, the generalizability of ADCL and EGSR to other complex reasoning tasks (e.g., commonsense reasoning, logical inference) requires further investigation. The mathematical problems in this paper share a common structure that might impact the performance of the tested techniques.
*   **Implementation Complexity:** Implementing ADCL and especially EGSR, with its guided trajectory generation and need to avoid distribution shifts in the EGSR, can be complex and require careful tuning. This might present a barrier to adoption for researchers and practitioners.
*   **Limited Ablation:** While the paper includes comparisons to several baselines, a more detailed ablation study that analyzes the individual contribution of different components within ADCL and EGSR would strengthen the analysis.  For example, it would be useful to know how much performance comes from simply adding more training data in the initial 0-accuracy set vs carefully guided self-reformulation in the EGSR case.
*   **"Near on-policy" assumption:** The paper argues that ESGR allows near on-policy updates because the generated trajectories are aligned with the model. However, without an ablation that analyzes various levels of perturbations (e.g., adding small random modifications to the expert's reasoning), the effect of how closely aligned the expert demonstration trajectory has to be is unknown.
*   **Effect Size**: The results showed marginal gains in some benchmarks (0.8% in SFT) and stronger effects in other benchmarks (+16% in EGSR(s, a)), suggesting it may be dataset dependent.

**Significance:**

The paper addresses important challenges in LLM training and presents practical techniques to enhance reasoning capabilities. ADCL provides a dynamic curriculum learning strategy that can potentially improve learning efficiency and robustness, while EGSR offers a promising approach to internalizing expert knowledge. The insights gained from this research could contribute to the development of more powerful and reliable LLMs for a wide range of applications. The EGSR technique, especially, represents a useful way of including expert demonstrations in the training of LLMs.

**Score:** 7.5

**Justification:**

The paper offers novelty in the form of two well-motivated training strategies that successfully address key challenges in LLM reasoning. The empirical results are compelling, demonstrating significant performance gains on challenging benchmarks. However, the computational cost of the proposed techniques, dependence on expert data quality, and lack of detailed ablation limit the overall impact. The contributions are well-formulated and demonstrate a good understanding of current challenges in language model training. The improvements relative to strong baselines suggest a promising step toward improved reasoning in LLMs, and the proposed techniques represent viable approaches to improve performance on LLMs that make reasoning-based errors.

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### Adaptive Diffusion Policy Optimization for Robotic Manipulation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.08376v1)
- **Authors**: Huiyun Jiang, Zhuang Yang
- **Abstract**: Recent studies have shown the great potential of diffusion models in improving reinforcement learning (RL) by modeling complex policies, expressing a high degree of multi-modality, and efficiently handling high-dimensional continuous control tasks. However, there is currently limited research on how to optimize diffusion-based polices (e.g., Diffusion Policy) fast and stably. In this paper, we propose an Adam-based Diffusion Policy Optimization (ADPO), a fast algorithmic framework containing best practices for fine-tuning diffusion-based polices in robotic control tasks using the adaptive gradient descent method in RL. Adaptive gradient method is less studied in training RL, let alone diffusion-based policies. We confirm that ADPO outperforms other diffusion-based RL methods in terms of overall effectiveness for fine-tuning on standard robotic tasks. Concretely, we conduct extensive experiments on standard robotic control tasks to test ADPO, where, particularly, six popular diffusion-based RL methods are provided as benchmark methods. Experimental results show that ADPO acquires better or comparable performance than the baseline methods. Finally, we systematically analyze the sensitivity of multiple hyperparameters in standard robotics tasks, providing guidance for subsequent practical applications. Our video demonstrations are released in https://github.com/Timeless-lab/ADPO.git.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Adam-based Diffusion Policy Optimization (ADPO), an algorithmic framework aimed at improving the speed and stability of fine-tuning diffusion-based policies for robotic control tasks. ADPO leverages adaptive gradient descent methods, specifically incorporating a discount factor into the adaptive gradient to enhance policy optimization. The paper compares ADPO against several existing diffusion-based RL methods across standard robotic control tasks.  Experimental results demonstrate that ADPO achieves better or comparable performance, along with improved training stability. The paper also includes a sensitivity analysis of hyperparameters to provide guidance for practical applications. The authors provide code and video demonstrations.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The central idea of applying an Adam-based adaptive policy gradient method (ADAPG) to diffusion-based RL is a reasonable incremental step, but not highly groundbreaking. While the paper claims to be the first to leverage ADAPG for diffusion-based RL, the combination of existing techniques lowers its overall novelty. Adaptive gradient methods are well-established in deep learning, and their application to RL is not entirely novel. The paper primarily focuses on the specific implementation within the diffusion policy optimization framework. The introduction of the discount factor is a sensible adaptation but lacks substantial theoretical justification in the context of diffusion policies.

*   **Significance:** The significance of the work hinges on the empirical results demonstrating improved performance and stability. The experimental evaluation, comparing against several benchmark methods, does lend credence to the effectiveness of ADPO. The sensitivity analysis of hyperparameters provides useful practical insights. However, the observed improvements seem to be task-specific, and it’s not clear if ADPO offers a generalizable solution for all diffusion-based RL challenges. The paper provides no theoretical analysis to justify the observed improvements or to predict the scenarios where ADPO will be most effective. Without any theoretical underpinnings, it is difficult to assess the long-term impact of this method.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Comprehensive experimental evaluation: The paper includes comparisons against several existing methods on various robotic tasks.
    *   Practical insights: The sensitivity analysis of hyperparameters provides valuable guidance for practitioners.
    *   Code and video demonstrations are provided for reproducibility.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Incremental novelty: The core idea builds upon existing techniques.
    *   Lack of theoretical justification: The paper lacks a formal analysis to explain the effectiveness of ADPO.
    *   Limited generalizability: The improvements appear to be task-specific.
    *   The paper doesn't investigate more recent developments in the diffusion model realm (e.g., consistency models)
    *   The sensitivity analysis is descriptive but doesn't explain the "why" behind hyperparameter choices.
    *   Presentation could be improved. The paper is not always clear on the specific details of how ADAPG is integrated into the diffusion policy.

**Overall:**

The paper presents a useful engineering contribution by demonstrating the effectiveness of Adam-based optimization for diffusion policies in robotic control. The improvements in training stability are valuable. However, the incremental nature of the novelty and the lack of theoretical grounding limit its overall impact. The paper would be significantly strengthened by a more in-depth analysis of why ADPO works and a more detailed investigation of its limitations.

**Score: 6**

**Rationale:** The paper introduces an approach with a reasonable empirical performance, but the limited novelty and lack of theoretical analysis prevent it from being a truly exceptional contribution. While the experimental results show improvements in stability and performance compared to existing methods, the approach is not drastically different from what is already available. Without a theoretical foundation, it is difficult to determine the approach's true potential and guide further research. Therefore, a score of 6 reflects the paper's value as an incremental improvement with practical benefits but also acknowledges its shortcomings in terms of novelty and theoretical rigor.

- **Classification**: cs.RO
- **Score**: 6/10

### Towards Contamination Resistant Benchmarks
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.08389v1)
- **Authors**: Rahmatullah Musawi, Sheng Lu
- **Abstract**: The rapid development of large language models (LLMs) has transformed the landscape of natural language processing. Evaluating LLMs properly is crucial for understanding their potential and addressing concerns such as safety. However, LLM evaluation is confronted by various factors, among which contamination stands out as a key issue that undermines the reliability of evaluations. In this work, we introduce the concept of contamination resistance to address this challenge. We propose a benchmark based on Caesar ciphers (e.g., "ab" to "bc" when the shift is 1), which, despite its simplicity, is an excellent example of a contamination resistant benchmark. We test this benchmark on widely used LLMs under various settings, and we find that these models struggle with this benchmark when contamination is controlled. Our findings reveal issues in current LLMs and raise important questions regarding their true capabilities. Our work contributes to the development of contamination resistant benchmarks, enabling more rigorous LLM evaluation and offering insights into the true capabilities and limitations of LLMs.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Towards Contamination Resistant Benchmarks":

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the critical issue of data contamination in evaluating large language models (LLMs). It introduces the concept of "contamination resistance" as a desirable characteristic for benchmarks.  The authors propose using Caesar ciphers as a contamination-resistant benchmark due to their inherent dynamism, ability to assess several skills (deduction, arithmetic, generalization), ease of update, and consistent complexity.  They test several LLMs on Caesar cipher tasks under various settings and demonstrate that these models, despite their apparent prowess in other areas, struggle with Caesar ciphers when contamination is controlled. This highlights inconsistencies in LLM competence and questions their true capabilities.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The core idea of using Caesar ciphers as a contamination-resistant benchmark is **moderately novel**.  While ciphers and simple transformations are sometimes used in educational settings, the paper formalizes their use in evaluating LLMs specifically through the lens of contamination resistance.  The definition of "contamination resistance" itself is a valuable contribution.

**Significance:** The paper's significance is in its focus on **contamination**. Demonstrating vulnerabilities in LLM evaluation is important.  However, the choice of Caesar ciphers might be seen as too simplistic. While the authors argue that this simplicity is advantageous, it raises questions about whether failures on this task truly reflect broader limitations in reasoning or simply a lack of optimization for this specific, elementary skill. The paper adequately reveals the problem but does not offer a practical solution beyond the idea of using simple ciphers. The empirical section has weaknesses. Only one model (GPT-4) demonstrates any success. It would significantly strengthen the argument for contamination if other models had prior success or displayed some capabilities. The analysis of different prompts and their effects is interesting, but a broader investigation beyond the models already tested is needed. The conclusions from their limited tests could be overstated.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear Definition of Contamination Resistance:** The paper provides a clear and useful definition of what constitutes a contamination-resistant benchmark.
*   **Highlighting a Persistent Problem:** The paper effectively highlights the challenges of contamination in LLM evaluation, a significant problem.
*   **Simplicity and Scalability:** The Caesar cipher benchmark is easy to implement, scale, and update, making it practically useful.
*   **Directly Tackles a Validity Issue**: It directly challenges the conclusions drawn from benchmarking tasks when the benchmark may be invalidated by test-data leaks.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Oversimplification of LLM Abilities:** A failure on Caesar ciphers might not necessarily translate into general limitations in reasoning or generalization. LLMs are trained to perform a wide variety of tasks and might simply lack specific optimization for this simple encryption method.
*   **Limited Empirical Evaluation:** A wider range of LLMs and experiments with different cipher settings (e.g. different alphabets, shift values, or longer phrases) could provide a stronger case.
*   **Lack of Comparative Benchmarks:** The paper doesn't compare this benchmark to other similar approaches to contamination resistance. It might benefit from discussing other dynamic benchmarking strategies.
*   **Lack of Solutions**: The paper mostly sheds light on the problem, however, does not offer any practical approaches to make the benchmark resistant beyond using the Caesar Cipher.

**Potential Influence:** The paper could influence the way LLM benchmarks are designed, prompting researchers to think more critically about data contamination. However, its effect could be limited if other, more robust contamination-resistant techniques emerge.

**Rigorous Rationale for Score:** The paper's significance is primarily in its framing of the contamination problem and in proposing the Caesar cipher as a simple and dynamic test. However, this choice of a very simple test as the primary example limits the scope of the claims.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### Accelerating Chain-of-Thought Reasoning: When Goal-Gradient Importance Meets Dynamic Skipping
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.08392v1)
- **Authors**: Ren Zhuang, Ben Wang, Shuifa Sun
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models leverage Chain-of-Thought (CoT) prompting for complex tasks, but their reasoning traces are often excessively verbose and inefficient, leading to significant computational costs and latency. Current CoT compression techniques typically rely on generic importance metrics and static compression rates, which may inadvertently remove functionally critical tokens or fail to adapt to varying reasoning complexity. To overcome these limitations, we propose Adaptive GoGI-Skip, a novel framework learning dynamic CoT compression via supervised fine-tuning. This approach introduces two synergistic innovations: (1) Goal-Gradient Importance (GoGI), a novel metric accurately identifying functionally relevant tokens by measuring the gradient influence of their intermediate representations on the final answer loss, and (2) Adaptive Dynamic Skipping (ADS), a mechanism dynamically regulating the compression rate based on runtime model uncertainty while ensuring local coherence through an adaptive N-token constraint. To our knowledge, this is the first work unifying a goal-oriented, gradient-based importance metric with dynamic, uncertainty-aware skipping for CoT compression. Trained on compressed MATH data, Adaptive GoGI-Skip demonstrates strong cross-domain generalization across diverse reasoning benchmarks including AIME, GPQA, and GSM8K. It achieves substantial efficiency gains - reducing CoT token counts by over 45% on average and delivering 1.6-2.0 times inference speedups - while maintaining high reasoning accuracy. Notably, it significantly outperforms existing baselines by preserving accuracy even at high effective compression rates, advancing the state of the art in the CoT reasoning efficiency-accuracy trade-off.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Accelerating Chain-of-Thought Reasoning: When Goal-Gradient Importance Meets Dynamic Skipping":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Adaptive GoGI-Skip, a novel framework for dynamically compressing Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning in Large Language Models (LLMs) via supervised fine-tuning. It tackles the inefficiency of standard CoT prompting by introducing two key innovations:

1.  **Goal-Gradient Importance (GoGI):** A metric that measures a token's functional relevance by quantifying the gradient influence of its intermediate representations on the final answer loss.  This aims to identify and preserve tokens critical for accurate reasoning, even if they seem semantically simple.

2.  **Adaptive Dynamic Skipping (ADS):** A mechanism that dynamically adjusts the compression rate based on runtime model uncertainty, as measured by predictive entropy. ADS employs Entropy-Driven Rate (EDR) regulation to adjust token retention and an Adaptive N-Constraint (ANC) to maintain local coherence by limiting consecutive deletions based on contextual complexity.

The authors train Adaptive GoGI-Skip on compressed MATH data and demonstrate its effectiveness in cross-domain generalization across various reasoning benchmarks (AIME, GPQA, GSM8K). The results highlight significant efficiency gains (reducing CoT token counts by over 45% on average) and inference speedups (1.6x-2.0x), while maintaining or even slightly improving reasoning accuracy compared to existing CoT compression techniques.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary strength lies in its novel combination of ideas. While individual concepts like gradient-based importance metrics or dynamic compression have been explored previously, the synergistic integration of GoGI (a goal-oriented, gradient-based metric) with ADS (a dynamic, uncertainty-aware skipping mechanism) is a significant contribution. It addresses key limitations of existing CoT compression methods, which often rely on generic importance metrics and static compression rates, leading to potential loss of crucial tokens or inability to adapt to varying reasoning complexities. The first-of-its-kind unified framework adds to the innovative value.

*   **Significance:** The work addresses a pressing problem in LLM research: the high computational cost and latency associated with CoT reasoning.  By significantly compressing CoT sequences without sacrificing accuracy, the paper paves the way for more efficient and practical deployment of LLMs for complex tasks.  The cross-domain generalization results demonstrate the framework's robustness and potential for wider applicability.  The approach's ability to preserve or improve accuracy at high compression rates is particularly noteworthy, potentially surpassing the fundamental limitations of static compression techniques.  The ablation studies convincingly showcase the complementary contributions of GoGI, EDR, and ANC. By enabling LLMs to reach a more 'thinking-optimal' state, the significance of the work is apparent.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Strong empirical evaluation across diverse benchmarks and model sizes.
    *   Well-reasoned design choices, with clear justifications for each component of the framework.
    *   Thorough ablation studies that demonstrate the individual contributions of each component.
    *   Detailed analysis of the framework's behavior, providing insights into its inner workings.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The Adaptive Parameter Tuner, while designed for reducing manual tuning, has details omitted. This lack of detail makes it harder to reproduce and fully assess the adaptive benefits.
    *   The offline nature of the compression might limit its applicability in certain real-time scenarios. Though well-justified, its a theoretical constraint that should be noted.
    *   The dependency on supervised fine-tuning might introduce a bias towards the training data.  Further investigation into the framework's performance on out-of-distribution examples is warranted. The MATH training dataset used might limit generalizability, making additional studies beneficial.

*   **Potential Influence:** The Adaptive GoGI-Skip framework has the potential to influence future research on LLM efficiency, particularly in the context of reasoning tasks.  It offers a compelling alternative to static compression techniques and motivates further exploration of dynamic, context-aware compression strategies. The framework may also inspire the development of new importance metrics that are specifically tailored to different reasoning tasks and model architectures. The exploration of Reinforcement Learning (RL) for end-to-end learned efficiency is also noteworthy, showing future work in improving CoT efficiency.

*   **Rigorous Rationale:** The combination of a gradient-based importance metric with a dynamic skipping strategy is a unique contribution that addresses the limitations of existing CoT compression techniques. While individual components have some degree of prior art, their combination and the thorough empirical evaluation of the resulting framework justify a high score. The framework's practical significance, as evidenced by its ability to significantly reduce CoT token counts without sacrificing accuracy, further supports this rating. The potential for Adaptive GoGI-Skip to inspire future research on LLM efficiency and its demonstrated robustness and scalability make it a notable advance in the field. However, some omissions of algorithmic details for the adaptive parameter tuning as well as the offline implementation hinder full reproducibility, somewhat tempering the significance.

**Score: 8**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### TUMS: Enhancing Tool-use Abilities of LLMs with Multi-structure Handlers
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.08402v1)
- **Authors**: Aiyao He, Sijia Cui, Shuai Xu, Yanna Wang, Bo Xu
- **Abstract**: Recently, large language models(LLMs) have played an increasingly important role in solving a wide range of NLP tasks, leveraging their capabilities of natural language understanding and generating. Integration with external tools further enhances LLMs' effectiveness, providing more precise, timely, and specialized responses. However, LLMs still encounter difficulties with non-executable actions and improper actions, which are primarily attributed to incorrect parameters. The process of generating parameters by LLMs is confined to the tool level, employing the coarse-grained strategy without considering the different difficulties of various tools. To address this issue, we propose TUMS, a novel framework designed to enhance the tool-use capabilities of LLMs by transforming tool-level processing into parameter-level processing. Specifically, our framework consists of four key components: (1) an intent recognizer that identifies the user's intent to help LLMs better understand the task; (2) a task decomposer that breaks down complex tasks into simpler subtasks, each involving a tool call; (3) a subtask processor equipped with multi-structure handlers to generate accurate parameters; and (4) an executor. Our empirical studies have evidenced the effectiveness and efficiency of the TUMS framework with an average of 19.6\% and 50.6\% improvement separately on easy and hard benchmarks of ToolQA, meanwhile, we demonstrated the key contribution of each part with ablation experiments, offering more insights and stimulating future research on Tool-augmented LLMs.
- **Summary**: Okay, I can provide a summary and critical evaluation of the paper based on the provided text.

**Summary:**

The paper "TUMS: Enhancing Tool-use Abilities of LLMs with Multi-structure Handlers" addresses the limitations of Large Language Models (LLMs) in tool use, specifically concerning the accuracy of parameter generation. The authors argue that current LLMs treat all tools the same way, regardless of their complexity, leading to non-executable or improper actions.  To overcome this, the paper introduces TUMS, a framework that enhances LLMs' tool-use capabilities by shifting the focus from tool-level processing to parameter-level processing. TUMS consists of four modules: an intent recognizer, a task decomposer, a subtask processor equipped with multi-structure handlers, and an executor. The multi-structure handlers are designed to generate accurate parameters by considering the different difficulties associated with various tools.  Experiments on the ToolQA benchmark demonstrate the effectiveness of TUMS, with significant improvements over baseline methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its **parameter-level processing** approach to tool use. Most existing works focus on tool selection, planning, or self-verification at the *tool* level. The idea of tailoring parameter generation based on the complexity and parameter characteristics of *individual* tools is a valuable contribution. The multi-structure handlers are a concrete implementation of this concept. The integration of the intent recognizer is a nice touch, adding a layer of context awareness. However, intent recognition and task decomposition are not new in themselves.
*   **Significance:** The improvements reported on the ToolQA benchmark, especially on the harder questions, are significant. The ablation studies, particularly those comparing TUMS with and without multi-structure handlers, provide evidence for the importance of the proposed technique. Demonstrating that a more nuanced approach to parameter generation yields better results could influence future research directions in tool-augmented LLMs. Specifically, the improvement in hard examples demonstrate the ability of the model to deal with a harder tasks in comparison with prior models.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clearly Defined Problem:** The paper identifies a specific and important limitation of LLMs in tool use.
    *   **Well-Structured Framework:** TUMS is logically organized and well-explained.
    *   **Empirical Validation:**  The experiments are conducted on a established benchmark (ToolQA), and the results are convincing. The ablation studies are particularly useful for understanding the contribution of each component.
    *   **Clear Explanation and Writing:** Overall the paper is well explained and understandable.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Reliance on a Single Benchmark:** While ToolQA is a useful benchmark, evaluating TUMS on a wider range of datasets or real-world applications would further strengthen the claims.
    *   **Limited Discussion of Computational Cost:** While the ablation study touches on efficiency, a more detailed analysis of the computational overhead of TUMS compared to baseline methods is needed. How practical is TUMS for real-time applications?
    *   **Scalability:** Does the performance degrades as the number of tools increases.
    *   **Lack of Implementation Details:** Provide an open-source code base to better understand the nuances of the method.
    *   **Scope and Generalizability:** Although the results are promising on ToolQA, the specific structure handlers are likely tailored for that dataset, limiting wider application without major modifications. The question then becomes: how difficult is it to adapt the approach to new tools and datasets?
*   **Potential Influence:** This paper has the potential to shift the focus of research on tool-augmented LLMs toward more fine-grained parameter generation strategies. It also offers a valuable framework that can be extended with new tool types and applications.

**Justification for Score:**

I am assigning a score of **7** out of 10.

*   The paper introduces a novel approach to parameter generation for tool-augmented LLMs and demonstrates its effectiveness on a established benchmark.
*   The multi-structure handlers are a unique contribution, and the experimental results provide strong support for their importance.
*   The paper is well-written and clearly explains the proposed framework.
*   However, the reliance on a single benchmark, the limited discussion of computational cost, scope, and lack of implementation details prevents it from receiving a higher score.
*   The score reflects its contribution to the community as it motivates the community to think deeper about the parameter generation task rather than tool selections.
